II. 
Overview of CES Group 
14 
Q.  Please explain the function of Customer Energy Solutions 
15 
(“CES”). 
16 
A. 
CES develops, implements, and operates the programs, tools, 
17 
and customer support technologies for the clean energy 
18 
transition. CES also integrates distributed energy resources 
19 


--- Page 12 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
12 
 
(“DER”) into the Company’s energy systems and plans to 
1 
address future infrastructure needs. Groups within CES 
2 
implement the Company’s EE, demand management (“DM”), NWS, 
3 
NPS, electric vehicle (“EV”), energy storage, distributed 
4 
energy, demonstration projects, rate design, DSP, AMI, and 
5 
new Customer Service System (“CSS”) development. 
6 
Q.  How 
is 
CES 
structured 
and 
what 
are 
the 
general 
7 
responsibilities of each function? 
8 
A.  CES is structured as follows: 
9 
 
10 
 
Within the Energy Efficiency & Distribution Resource 
11 
Planning group, the Demand Management team implements the 
12 
Company’s Demand Response, NWS, and NPS programs, as 
13 
authorized through a variety of proceedings. The Distributed 
14 
Resource Planning group plans upgrades to the Company’s 
15 


--- Page 13 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
13 
 
electric distribution system, implements the DSP, and 
1 
supports customers installing DER. The E-Mobility and 
2 
Demonstration Projects group implements the EV Charging Make 
3 
Ready program authorized in the Proceeding on Motion of the 
4 
Commission Regarding Electric Vehicle Supply Equipment and 
5 
Infrastructure1 (the “EVSE Proceeding”), managed charging, 
6 
and the Company’s demonstration projects as authorized in 
7 
the Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to 
8 
Reforming the Energy Vision (“REV”).2 The EE group implements 
9 
EE and heating electrification programs authorized via the 
10 
proceeding In the Matter of a Comprehensive Energy 
11 
Efficiency Initiative3 (the “New Efficiency New York 
12 
Proceeding” or “NENY Proceeding”). 
13 
 
1 Case 18-E-0138, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission Regarding Electric 
Vehicle Supply Equipment and Infrastructure. 
2 Case 14-M-0101, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to 
Reforming the Energy Vision. 
3 Case 18-M-0084, In the Matter of a Comprehensive Energy Efficiency 
Initiative. 


--- Page 14 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
14 
 
 
Rate Engineering maintains the Company’s tariffs, calculates 
1 
rates, and conducts cost of service and cost allocation 
2 
studies for electric, gas, and steam. 
3 
 
The Statewide Energy Data Integration group works with New 
4 
York State Department of Public Service (“DPS”) Staff and 
5 
stakeholders to develop, provide, and maintain energy data 
6 
for third party use through the New York Public Service 
7 
Commission’s (“PSC”) Proceeding on Motion of the Commission 
8 
Regarding Strategic Use of Energy Related Data.4 
9 
 
The Customer Service System (“CSS”) group oversees the 
10 
design, build, and implementation of the Company’s new 
11 
customer service system. Once complete, the ongoing 
12 
management of this system will move to Customer Operations. 
13 
Since the Company will launch the new CSS during the rate 
14 
year, program changes for CSS-related investments are 
15 
discussed in the Customer Operations Panel.  
16 
 
4 Case 20-M-0082, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission Regarding Strategic 
Use of Energy Related Data. 


--- Page 15 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
15 
 
The AMI group handles the Company’s roll out of advanced 
1 
meters and metering systems. 
2 
The Utility of the Future group is responsible for 
3 
developing new business models and compensation mechanisms 
4 
to promote the distributed and clean energy resources 
5 
necessary to meet the State’s policy goals. Specifically, 
6 
the group advocates for and oversees the implementation of 
7 
incentives for clean and renewable resources eligible for 
8 
Value of Distributed Energy Resources (“VDER”) and Remote 
9 
Crediting compensation and for procuring energy storage 
10 
projects, including the bulk storage procurements authorized 
11 
via the proceeding In the Matter of Energy Storage 
12 
Deployment Program5 (the “Energy Storage Proceeding”).  
13 
III. Purpose of Testimony 
14 
Q. 
What is the purpose of this testimony? 
15 
A. 
This testimony proposes programs and investments to advance 
16 
achievement 
of 
New 
York 
State 
clean 
energy 
policy 
17 
 
5 Case 18-E-0130, In the Matter of Energy Storage Deployment Program. 


--- Page 16 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
16 
 
objectives, which are aligned with similar City of New York 
1 
objectives. Our proposals accomplish these objectives by 
2 
integrating DER into our energy systems, engaging customers 
3 
to manage their energy use, expanding clean energy access 
4 
in Disadvantaged Communities and for low- and moderate-
5 
income customers (“LMI”), and supporting our energy systems’ 
6 
resilience and reliability. These proposals are incremental 
7 
to funds previously authorized for our other clean energy 
8 
programs. Examples of the proposed investments include: 
9 
 Make-ready programs that will offset the costs of 
10 
customer behind-the-meter electrical work needed to 
11 
enable electrification of heating and appliances, and 
12 
electrical 
work 
needed 
to 
integrate 
DER 
in 
13 
Disadvantaged Communities; 
14 
 Customer tools that will help customers understand 
15 
clean energy options, including the costs and benefits 
16 
of these options, and engage the market to adopt the 
17 
solutions; 
18 


--- Page 17 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
17 
 
 A renewable solar generation program that will stream 
1 
benefits to low-income customers through a bill credit; 
2 
and 
3 
 Alternative infrastructure programs that will advance 
4 
the integration of DER into our energy systems and 
5 
maximize the benefits they bring to all customers. 
6 
Q. 
Why are these investments necessary? 
7 
A. 
The CLCPA Panel describes the Company’s support for New 
8 
York’s clean energy goals and its Clean Energy Commitment. 
9 
Achieving the targets outlined in the Climate Leadership and 
10 
Community Protection Act (“CLCPA”) legislation requires Con 
11 
Edison to play a leading role in partnering with 
12 
stakeholders to collectively invest in a low-carbon future.  
13 
Over the past several years, the clean energy market in New 
14 
York has made great strides. For example, between 2017-2021, 
15 
the Company worked with customers and the market to 
16 
interconnect 
220 
MW 
of 
solar, 
achieved 
incremental 
17 
reductions of over 2,400 GWh and 2.6 million Dth in EE, and 
18 
installed over 28,000 cold climate heat pumps through the 
19 
NYS Clean Heat program. The next few years will be crucial 
20 


--- Page 18 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
18 
 
to accelerate this growth for an effective transition. Our 
1 
proposed investments support customers and third-party clean 
2 
energy businesses by (1) reducing upfront costs of clean 
3 
energy projects; (2) facilitating solutions; and (3) 
4 
engaging Disadvantaged Communities and low-income customers 
5 
in the clean energy transition.  
6 
Q.  What period does this testimony cover? 
7 
A. 
The twelve-month period ending December 31, 2023 (“Rate 
8 
Year” or “RY1”) and, if there is a three-year rate plan, the 
9 
twelve-month periods ending December 31, 2024 (“RY2”) and 
10 
December 31, 2025 (“RY3”). 
11 
Q. 
Please summarize the programs outlined in this testimony and 
12 
their associated costs. 
13 
A. 
We propose capital funding of $165.7 million for RY1, $246.4 
14 
million in RY2, and $236.1 million in RY3. The proposed 
15 
capital funding includes projects that are detailed either 
16 
in this testimony or in exhibits in the IT panel (RY1 $90.6 
17 
million, RY2 $89.9 million, and RY3 $89.4 million). 
18

Q. 
Please quickly explain what is meant by Regulatory Asset 
5 
treatment. 
6 
A. 
A regulatory asset is cost recovery authorized by a 
7 
regulatory agency that allows a utility to capitalize a cost 
8 
that would otherwise be expensed and recover it over time. 
9 
For the above programs that help customers offset the costs 
10 
of capital upgrades that aid the clean energy transition, 
11 
treating program costs similar to capital investments 
12 
advances 
policy 
objectives 
and/or 
provides 
customer 
13 
benefits. These include moderation of customer bill impacts 
14 
through amortization of costs to align customer costs with 
15 
the realization of customer benefits over time. The 
16 


--- Page 24 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
24 
 
Commission has approved regulatory asset treatment for EE 
1 
in prior Con Edison rate cases, and in the Order Establishing 
2 
Electric Vehicle Infrastructure Make-Ready Program and Other 
3 
Programs.6

IV. 
Supporting Customer Participation in 
6 
the Clean Energy Transition 
7 
Q.  Please explain the “clean energy transition.” 
8 
A. 
By clean energy transition, we mean the State’s move away 
9 
from carbon-intensive energy to renewable energy and clean 
10 
energy technologies, like storage and EE. The Company 
11 
intends to help the State and the City of New York achieve 
12 
 
 
9 The Company forecasts $73 million of unspent funds authorized to fund NENY

their ambitious clean energy goals, as outlined in the 
1 
CLCPA, as well as New York City policies.10 Making and 
2 
ultimately achieving this transition and realizing its 
3 
benefits will require significant utility investment.  
4 
Q. 
How is the Company supporting customers in the clean energy 
5 
transition? 
6 
A. 
The Company supports customers’ transition to clean energy 
7 
through a variety of programs and offerings designed to help 
8 
them make informed decisions about their clean energy 
9 
options and reduce the upfront costs of installing clean 
10 
energy technologies. Current offerings include the Company’s 
11 
EE programs, heating electrification program, EV charging 
12 
make-ready 
program, 
managed 
charging 
program, 
demand 
13 
response offerings, energy storage solicitations, and 
14 
support for customers seeking to install on-site solar and 
15 
other DER. 
16 
We plan to increase our offerings during the next three 
17 
years. In this section, we discuss several programs to 
18 
 
10 E.g., New York City Local Law 66 of 2014.

3

4 
analyze their clean energy options, purchasing solar 
5

6

clean energy into energy systems.

schools, City-run community centers and housing shelters.

5 
A. Heating Electrification Make-Ready Program 
6 
Q. 
Please describe heating electrification and the transition 
7 
customers must undertake to move from fossil-fueled heating. 
8 
A. 
Currently, many customers heat their homes and businesses 
9 
with fossil fuels, like oil, propane, and natural gas. To 
10 
achieve CLCPA-mandated emissions reductions, one category 
11 
of electrification, efficient electric heat pumps for space 
12 
and water heating, must realize increased adoption rates 
13 
across New York State. Transitioning to electric heat pumps 
14 
generally involves making significant upgrades to a building 
15 
– including new heating equipment (e.g., a cold-climate heat 
16 
pump), and expanding and/or adding electrical boxes and 
17 
wiring to power the heat pumps.  
18 
Q. 
Does the Company currently support customers to install 
19 
efficient electric heat? 
20 


--- Page 30 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
30 
 
A. 
Yes. Con Edison offers incentives to customers for both cold 
1 
climate air-source and ground-source heat pumps through the 
2 
New York State Clean Heat program, a statewide program 
3 
developed jointly by the Department of Public Service, the 
4 
New York State Energy Research and Development Agency 
5 
(“NYSERDA”) and the state’s electric utilities.  
6 
Q. 
Please explain the Clean Heat program. 
7 
A. 
The New York State Clean Heat program incentivizes 
8 
contractors and customers to reduce the upfront cost to 
9 
customers of installing cold-climate-certified air-source 
10 
heat pumps (including mini splits), and ground-source heat 
11 
pumps for space and water heating. The program also supports 
12 
building envelope (e.g., insulation, air sealing) upgrades 
13 
to reduce the building space heating and cooling prior to 
14 
heat pump installation. The State’s electric utilities 
15 
operate this program statewide using a common application 
16 
and framework of program rules. 
17 
To qualify for incentives, contractors must design and 
18 
install heat pump systems to meet criteria that include 
19 
adequately sizing the heat pump system for the customers’ 
20 


--- Page 31 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
31 
 
needs. Incentives available through the Clean Heat program 
1 
are based on the size of the heat pump itself (or, the energy 
2 
savings it generates for large custom projects), and, in 
3 
many buildings, are insufficient to overcome the barrier of 
4 
the costs of customer-sided electrical upgrades needed to 
5 
power the new heat pump equipment.  
6 
Q.  Is the Company proposing a program, beyond the existing 
7 
Clean Heat program, to support customers transitioning to 
8 
electric heat pumps as their primary source of heating? 
9 
A.  Yes. In addition to the Clean Heat program, the Company 
10 
proposes to launch a Heating Electrification Make-Ready 
11 
program to help offset the costs of behind-the-meter 
12 
electrical upgrades required both to electrify space and/or 
13 
water heating in buildings, and prepare these buildings’ 
14 
electrical systems for full electrification. By addressing 
15 
this cost barrier, the program is intended to make 
16 
electrification feasible for buildings that would be 
17 
challenged to complete them otherwise. 
18 
Q. 
Has the Panel prepared an exhibit describing the Heating 
19 
Electrification Make-Ready program? 
20 


--- Page 32 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
32 
 
A. 
Yes. A 7-page document entitled Heating Electrification 
1 
Make-Ready Whitepaper, Exhibit___(CES-1), has been prepared 
2 
under our direction and supervision.  
3

5

7

9

10

11 
use of electricity from space heating, water heating, and 
12 
other appliance use. These proposed incentives reduce the 
13 
costs of these electrical upgrades for customers and 
14 
building owners. Eligible upgrades include new electric 
15 
panels or sub-panels, wiring upgrades, and other necessary 
16 
behind-the-meter electrical upgrades.  
17

3 
Q. 
Why is this program needed?  
4 
A. 
To understand the need for this program, we explain the need 
5 
by sector: a) Residential (1-4 family) homes and b) multi-
6 
family and commercial building sectors.  
7 
Q.  First, why is this program needed for the 1-4 family home 
8 
sector? 
9 
A.  Based on interviews with electric heat pump installers and 
10 
distributors, the Company estimates that 10% to 30% of 
11 
existing 1-4 family homes lack capacity on their existing 
12 
electric panel to connect electrified heating and other 
13 
appliances. To install a heat pump, some customers need to 
14 
replace their electric circuit breaker panel or add a sub-
15 
panel, adding approximately $700 to $2,500 to the project 
16 


--- Page 34 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
34 
 
cost. Other customers may need an electric service upgrade. 
1 
While the Company pays the costs of needed work on Company 
2 
equipment to bring upgraded service to the meter, service 
3 
upgrades can still result in customer behind-the-meter 
4 
electrical work and costs (e.g., new conduit and service 
5 
entrance wires, and additional electrician labor hours to 
6 
coordinate and complete the customer-sided requirements of 
7 
the upgrade). 
8 
Currently, the Clean Heat Program does not address these 
9 
costs. Without intervention, customers are likely either to 
10 
find transitioning to electric heat pumps cost-prohibitive 
11 
or will pursue a cheaper, limited electrical upgrade 
12 
solution, generally a smaller sub-panel that may not allow 
13 
for future electrification of other appliances or EV 
14 
chargers. The proposed incentives will: 1) encourage more 
15 
1-4 family homeowners to pursue electrification today, and 
16 
2) support 1-4 family homeowners to make the upgrades 
17 
necessary to support future full electrification of their 
18 
homes most cost-effectively.  
19 


--- Page 35 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
35 
 
Q.  And why is this program needed for the multifamily and 
1 
commercial building sector? 
2 
A.  In addition to the issues faced by 1-4 family homeowners, 
3 
multifamily and commercial property owners face other 
4 
issues. For example, electrical costs associated with heat 
5 
pump installation can increase substantially due to building 
6 
layout (e.g., location of condenser units in relation to 
7 
entry of electrical service), heat pump system design 
8 
choices (e.g., multiple distributed units vs. one central 
9 
system), and existing building conditions (e.g., condition 
10 
of existing wiring). Costly electrical room upgrades may 
11 
also be necessary, such as replacement and rewiring of the 
12 
existing electrical panel.  
13 
Additionally, some building owners may choose to electrify 
14 
their buildings in phases for a variety of reasons (e.g., 
15 
existing 
equipment 
lifecycles, 
tenant 
lease 
terms), 
16 
potentially requiring panel work be repeated each phase. 
17 
This approach is less efficient and, over time, more costly 
18 
than addressing the building electrical needs upfront in a 
19 


--- Page 36 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
36 
 
single comprehensive upgrade that “future proofs” the 
1 
building.  
2 
This program will help offset these costs that can otherwise 
3 
prevent heat pump projects from moving forward.  
4 
Q.  How many buildings would this program support?  
5 
A.  As noted in Exhibit __ (CES-1), the program would provide 
6 
support to more than 3,000 existing multi-family and 
7 
commercial buildings and 8,000 homes between 2023 and 2025, 
8 
increasing adoption of clean heating technologies in these 
9 
existing buildings. 
10 
Q. 
How does the Company plan to structure the incentives for 
11 
this program? 
12 
A. 
The proposed incentives will depend on the customer segment 
13 
and level of the clean heat upgrade. For those residential 
14 
customers seeking space and/or water heating only, the 
15 
customer would be eligible for up to $2,000 incentive for 
16 
market-rate customers (or an estimated $2,500 incentive for 
17 
LMI customers) to replace their home’s current electrical 
18 
panel with a larger electrical panel. To be eligible for the 
19 


--- Page 37 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
37 
 
incentive, the customer’s new panel needs to add sufficient 
1 
capacity to fully electrify the building, including capacity 
2 
for both the current project (space and/or water heating 
3 
electrification) and the future electrification of other 
4 
appliances (e.g., stove, clothes dryers).  
5 
Residential customers seeking to convert all fossil-fuel 
6 
appliances to electricity (e.g., stoves, clothes dryers) at 
7 
once would be eligible for an additional incentive 
8 
(incremental to the space/water heating incentive) of $1,000 
9 
for additional behind-the-meter electrical costs associated 
10 
with providing power to these new appliances. 
11 
Multifamily and commercial and industrial property owners 
12 
would be eligible for incentives covering up to 70% of total 
13 
project cost. For LMI-qualifying multifamily buildings per 
14 
existing 
NENY 
energy 
efficiency 
program 
definitions, 
15 
building owners would be eligible for incentives covering 
16 
up to 100% of total project cost. Eligible costs would 
17 
include work related to the wiring of heat pump equipment 
18 
(e.g., condensers, air-handlers, water heaters), central 
19 
electrical room upgrades, and any other customer work 
20 


--- Page 38 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
38 
 
associated with upgraded service. Costs associated with 
1 
preparing a building for future electrification of other 
2 
appliances would also be eligible for incentives.  
3 
Q. 
What 
are 
the 
costs 
associated 
with 
the 
Heating 
4

5

7

8

9 
Q. 
What amortization period does the Company propose for the 
10 
program costs? 
11 
A. 
The Company proposes to amortize costs over 15 years.  
12 
Q. 
Why is a 15-year amortization period appropriate for the 
13 
Heating Electrification Make-Ready program? 
14 
A. 
The clean energy benefits resulting from the program will 
15 
accrue over the useful life of the electrical upgrades and 
16 
the operation of the supported heat pumps. This amortization 
17 
period moderates customer bill impacts and aligns customer 
18 
costs with the realization of customer benefits over time. 
19 


--- Page 39 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
39 
 
As discussed later, the Company proposes a Clean Heat 
1 
amortization period of 15 years as well as the same 
2 
amortization period for the Heating Electrification Make-
3 
Ready program.  
4 
Q. 
Does this program address all potential barriers associated 
5 
with having buildings be “made ready” for electrification?  
6 
A. 
While this program addresses the cost barrier of customer 
7 
behind the meter electrical upgrades, there are other 
8 
barriers with making buildings ready for electrification 
9 
that may warrant additional intervention and engagement. 
10 
These include proactive upsizing of Con Edison equipment 
11 
serving 
a 
building 
planning 
phased 
electrification, 
12 
understanding when building electrification projects are 
13 
likely to trigger building service upgrades (to be addressed 
14 
through the Customer Recommendation and Analysis Tools 
15 
below), or incenting building work needed for heat pump 
16 
installations to be “made ready” for future efficient 
17 
integration with clean district heat sources and/or sinks. 
18 
Q. 
In implementing this program, can the Company rely on 
19 
program experience? 
20 


--- Page 40 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
40 
 
A. 
Yes. Con Edison’s recent experience administering the 
1 
Electric Vehicle Charging Make Ready program provides an 
2 
opportunity to leverage lessons learned to inform program 
3 
design and execution. Con Edison will also integrate the 
4 
Heating Electrification Make-Ready program with the existing 
5 
Clean Heat program to provide an integrated and streamlined 
6 
customer experience. 
7

8 
Q. 
Does the Company have a program to improve its customer 
9 
experience? 
10 
A. 
Yes. The Company’s Strategic Customer Experience (“Strategic 
11 
CX”) Portfolio will deliver a dynamic customer experience 
12 
to meet evolving customer and stakeholder expectations as 
13 
well as enable the Company to lead the transition to a clean 
14 
energy future. While this portfolio is discussed in the 
15 
Customer 
Operations 
Panel 
testimony, 
the 
Customer 
16 
Recommendation and Analysis Tools are being developed in 
17 
partnership with Customer Operations to assist the clean 
18

15

16 
income customers.

18

19

D. Clean Energy Credit for Low-Income Customers Program  
4 
Q.  Please explain the Panel’s proposed Clean Energy Credit for 
5 
Low-Income Customers (“CEC-LI”) program, and how would it 
6 
contribute to an equitable transition to the State’s clean 
7 
energy future. 
8 
A.  Through 
the proposed 
program, 
we 
will 
create 
a 
9 
sustainable source of revenues to fund clean energy credits 
10 
that will result in low-income customers sharing in the 
11 
benefits of the clean energy transition. The revenue to fund 
12 
the bill credit will be generated by the Company owning and 
13 
operating 
transmission-connected solar 
generators. 
The 
14 
Company will develop these solar generators within the State 
15 
to provide additional economic development benefits. The 
16 
Company will also develop these solar generators outside its 
17 
and O&R’s service territories to mitigate any potential 
18 
vertical market concerns arising from ownership of these 
19 
solar resources.  
20 


--- Page 53 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
53 
 
As 
described 
below, 
the 
Company 
will 
issue 
annual 
1 
solicitations to purchase up to 1,000 MW of new solar 
2 
generating projects over a ten-year period. The Company will 
3 
use the revenues from these solar projects to provide 
4 
electric bill credits to our low-income customers.  
5 
Q.  Please describe how the program will work. 
6 
A. 
The Company will issue a competitive request for proposals 
7 
annually for the ten years beginning in 2023 seeking to 
8 
acquire 100 MW of solar project(s) each year. We expect 
9 
these facilities to commence operations starting in 2024. 
10 
The winning bidder(s) will be contractually responsible for 
11 
designing, permitting, constructing, interconnecting, and 
12 
commissioning an operating solar facility. They would then 
13 
transfer the facility to Con Edison on the commercial 
14 
operation date.  
15 
The investment in each solar project will be treated as 
16 
utility plant and included in rate base. The Company will 
17 
sell the available energy from its plants into the market 
18 
through the New York Independent System Operator (“NYISO”). 
19 
The Company will receive market revenues from the project 
20 


--- Page 54 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
54 
 
from energy and capacity sales. The Company will also record 
1 
the prevailing market value of Renewable Energy Credits 
2 
(“RECs”)13 as project revenue.14 Any net revenues from the 
3 
generating assets (i.e., NYISO market revenues plus the 
4 
prevailing market value of the RECs15 minus solar facility 
5 
O&M costs) will fund an additional electric bill credit for 
6 
low-income customers enrolled in the Company’s low-income 
7 
affordability program.  
8 
Q. 
How will low-income customers benefit from this program? 
9 
A. 
The Company forecasts that revenues after operating expenses 
10 
from each 100 MW of renewables will fund approximately $7 
11 
 
13 A Renewable Energy Credit (REC) represents 1 MWh of electricity generated 
by a qualifying renewable energy project in New York. Each Load Serving 
Entity (including Energy Service Companies (ESCOs) and utilities) must 
purchase a quantity of RECs equivalent to a certain percentage of the total 
energy used by their customers in each year, as specified through the 
Commission’s Clean Energy Standard. See, Case 15-E-0302, Proceeding on 
Motion of the Commission to Implement a Large-Scale Renewable Program and a 
Clean Energy Standard. 
14 The Company would use the RECs the project generates to offset its 
obligation to procure RECs on behalf of its full-service customers. 
15 The prevailing market value of the RECs will be set at the REC price 
established by NYSERDA’s most recent solicitation for Large Scale Renewable 
projects. See, e.g., https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/All-Programs/Clean-Energy-
Standard/Renewable-Generators-and-Developers/RES-Tier-One-
Eligibility/Solicitations-for-Long-term-Contracts/2020-Solicitation-
Resources 


--- Page 55 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
55 
 
million a year in low-income credits. This means each 100 
1 
MW of in-service solar generation would provide an 
2 
incremental credit of about $15 per customer per year, once 
3 
operational. 
The 
Company 
forecasts 
that 
when 
fully 
4 
implemented over 10 years, the 1,000 MW of solar projects 
5 
will enable it to reduce bills for each recipient by an 
6 
average of $17 per month (roughly $200 per year), or an 
7 
approximately 14% reduction in the average electric bill for 
8 
our low-income customers.  
 
9 
Q.  Did the Company consider the Commission’s concerns about 
10 
utility ownership of generation?  
11 
A. 
Yes. First, as noted above, the Company plans to seek 
12 
projects located outside of the Con Edison and O&R service 
13 
territories. This should alleviate any concerns about 
14 
vertical market power because the assets would not be 
15 
impacted by operational decisions on Con Edison or O&R 
16 
transmission and distribution systems. Furthermore, because 
17 
they do not have variable operating costs, the Company would 
18 
bid the solar generation as a price taker and would not be 
19 
able to artificially impact wholesale market prices. 
20 


--- Page 56 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
56 
 
Moreover, the Commission has acknowledged the role for 
1 
utility investment to address particular areas, such as 
2 
meeting low-income customer needs where there is no 
3 
developed market and the public interest warrants utility 
4 
investment to support development.16 Thus, the Commission 
5 
can approve utility ownership where there is a public 
6 
benefit, such as the advancement of the State’s renewable 
7 
energy goals and the support for energy affordability.17  
8 
Q. 
Why is this proposal better than having third parties own 
9 
and operate solar facilities? 
10 
A. 
The Company’s proposal has two significant benefits that 
11 
would not necessarily be replicable without utility 
12 
ownership of the asset. The first benefit is that as a 
13 
regulated entity, we can stream all net market revenues to 
14 
our low-income customers to provide them incremental bill 
15 
 
16  
Case 14-M-0101, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to 
Reforming the Energy Vision, Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility Revenue 
Model Policy Framework (issued May 19, 2016), p. 49. 
17  
Case 14-M-0101, Proceeding on Motion of the Commission in Regard to 
Reforming the Energy Vision, Order Adopting a Ratemaking and Utility Revenue 
Model Policy Framework (issued May 19, 2016), p. 54. 


--- Page 57 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
57 
 
credits. The second benefit has to do with the residual 
1 
value of the project. When it owns the asset, the Company 
2 
can continue to operate the asset for customer benefits for 
3 
the life of the asset and potentially longer, if repowered. 
4 
With this residual value, our customers will potentially pay 
5 
less than the cost of obtaining the renewable power from a 
6 
third party for the full life of the asset.  
7 
Q. 
Please describe the funding requirements of this program 
8 
during the rate years, as well as the proposed ratemaking 
9 
treatment for this program. 
10 
A.  The estimated incremental costs are for purchasing and 
11 
owning the proposed 200 MW of solar resources that would 
12 
become operational by 2025. We forecast $100 million in each 
13 
of 2024-2025 to fund these plants.  
14 
Upon commercial operation, the Company will include its 
15 
payment to purchase the solar resources in rate base. 
16 
Energy, capacity, and REC revenues will offset the O&M costs 
17 
associated with these renewable resources.  
18 
Q. 
Can the Company use tax benefits available to other 
19 
developers to reduce the cost of these projects? 
20 


--- Page 58 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
58 
 
A. 
The Company will attempt to avail itself of any applicable 
1 
tax benefits.  
2 
Q. 
Is the Company’s proposal compatible with New York’s 10 GW 
3 
Distributed Solar Roadmap (“Roadmap”) issued by Staff and 
4 
NYSERDA on December 17, 2021? 
5 
A. 
Yes. By targeting larger transmission scale projects and 
6 
streaming the net benefits of operating those projects to 
7 
all our low-income customers, the Company’s proposal fills 
8 
a need not covered by the Roadmap. The Roadmap proposes 
9 
meaningful incentives to encourage both direct adoption of 
10 
solar by low-income customers and customers participating 
11 
in CDG projects, but does not provide incentives for the 
12 
larger solar systems the Company would acquire under its 
13 
CEC-LI program. Similarly, while the Roadmap targets 150 MW 
14 
of rooftop installation and 300 MW CDG, with a target of 40 
15 
percent participation from low-income customers, most low-
16 
income customers are not able to directly install rooftop 
17 
at 
their 
premises. 
Moreover, 
low-income 
customers 
18 
subscribing in a CDG project would only be expected to 
19 
receive a 10% discount on their solar allocation. Therefore, 
20 


--- Page 59 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
59 
 
the Company’s proposal, which will provide all low-income 
1 
customers with a larger, more meaningful electric bill 
2 
credit, complements the Roadmap.  
3

4 
program? 
5

6

7 
been prepared under our direction and supervision.

E. Distributed System Platform 
10 
Q. 
What is the Distributed System Platform (DSP)?  
11 
A.  The Company’s DSP is the platform of tools, technology, 
12 
infrastructure, 
and 
services 
that 
the 
Company 
is 
13 
implementing to more fully integrate DER into electric 
14 
distribution system planning and operations. DSPs are 
15 
comprised of the people, processes, and systems that allow 
16 
utilities to provide three core, interrelated services: (1) 
17 
facilitating 
expanded 
DER 
integration; 
(2) 
sharing 
18 
information that helps third parties identify business 
19 
opportunities and maximize the value of their investments; 
20 


--- Page 60 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
60 
 
and (3) expanding market services. The evolution of the 
1 
Company’s DSP since 2016 is documented in its bi-annual 
2 
filings of its Distributed System Implementation Plan, most 
3 
recently filed in June 2020.18 
4 
Beginning with project development and interconnection, our 
5 
proposed DSP improvements will allow DER developers greater 
6 
insights into customer and system needs – coupled with new 
7 
interconnection options that enable larger DER installations 
8 
at lower overall interconnection costs. The DSP will enable 
9 
a more dynamic operation of the distribution system, with 
10 
DER seamlessly responding to dispatch, operational control, 
11 
or price signals for real and/or reactive needs.

Q. 
What is a smart inverter? 
19 


--- Page 68 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
68 
 
A. 
An inverter is a device that converts power generated by a 
1 
clean energy installation from Direct Current to usable 
2 
Alternating Current so that it can be used by customers 
3 
within their home or business. A smart inverter allows the 
4 
remote monitoring and control of the power generated by the 
5 
clean energy installation. 
6 
Q. 
Does the Company propose any programs to address smart 
7 
inverters?  
8 
A.  Yes. The Smart Inverter customer program will spur adoption 
9 
of the latest clean energy technologies by providing 
10 
customer incentives to purchase these devices for their 
11 
solar installations. Additionally, this program will enable 
12 
programming of smart inverters’ reactive volt-VAR support 
13 
settings, and other grid support features creating potential 
14 
to call upon these resources to decongest and add capacity 
15 
to the system. 
16 
Q.  
Please explain Connect DER. 
17 
A.  
The Connect DER device allows for lower cost integration of 
18 
solar power into a premises’ electrical system. The Company 
19 
proposes to install these devices at 2,400 customer 
20 


--- Page 69 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
69 
 
locations to enhance remote monitoring and control of the 
1 
associated DER. The program is part of NYSERDA’s Future Grid 
2 
Challenge.20 
3 
Q.  
Please describe the Company’s proposed investments in 
4 
modernizing Network Protectors. 
5 
A.  
The Company will continue its existing program to modernize 
6 
Network Protectors (“NWP”) through upgrading relays and 
7 
installing 
Supervisory 
Control 
and 
Data 
Acquisition 
8 
(“SCADA”) capability. This grid modernization effort, which 
9 
begun in 2017, not only provides NWP with the intelligence 
10 
to distinguish DG back-feed from fault current through an 
11 
upgraded protective relay, but also permits remote control 
12 
and two-way power flow. Upgraded NWPs are required for the 
13 
system to accommodate clean DER that exports onto the 
14 
system. This program covers the cost of upgrading relays and 
15 
 
20 “Winning Teams Under First Two Rounds of ‘Future Grid Challenge’ 
Will Improve Efficiency and Reliability in Renewable Energy 
Integration,” NYSERDA press release (Issued March 26, 2020). See, 
https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/About/Newsroom/2020-Announcements/2020-03-
26-NYSERDA-ANNOUNCES-WINNERS-OF-CLEAN-ENERGY-CHALLENGE-TO-MODERNIZE-

installing SCADA, where necessary, within NWPs so that clean 
1 
DER developers do not incur these costs.  
2 
 
 
To date, the Company has upgraded 3,829 NWP (and equipped 
3 
1,336 of these with SCADA capability) out of approximately 
4 
27,000 NWP in the secondary grid. The Company plans to 
5 
continue this program to address each location that is or 
6 
becomes DG constrained, as well as all locations with 
7 
persistent back-feed issues.  
8 
Q. 
What is Conservation Voltage Optimization (“CVO”)? 
9 
A. 
The CVO program uses the Company’s AMI meters to precisely 
10 
measure the voltage being delivered across the system and 
11 
more closely align power flows to maintain acceptable 
12 
voltage along the distribution feeder. The CVO program 
13 
measures the complete voltage profile of a network area and 
14 
optimizes the operation of voltage regulation equipment 
15 
according to the measured voltage at the edge of the grid. 
16 
Using optimal voltages reduces total energy consumption and 
17 
associated power generation emissions, resulting in both 
18 
customer energy savings and a reduced carbon footprint. 
19 


--- Page 71 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
71 
 
Q. 
Has the Company been implementing CVO in conjunction with 
1 
its AMI program? 
2 
A. 
Yes. Con Edison has been implementing CVO incrementally 
3 
throughout the ongoing AMI meter deployment efforts. 
4 
Currently, 80 networks use CVO to optimize voltage. The 
5 
remaining two networks will optimize voltage during 2022. 
6 
Using this information, the Company has proactively changed 
7 
the voltage schedule to optimize CVO.  
8 
Q. 
As a result of the AMI program, does the Company have CVO 
9 
goals? 
10 
A. 
Yes. The Company’s AMI business case projected 1.50 percent 
11 
CVO energy savings. As of December 2021, we achieved 1.21 
12 
CVO energy savings and we expect to meet the 1.50 percent 
13 
CVO energy savings goals.  
14 
Q. 
Does the Company want to increase the level of CVO energy 
15 
savings? 
16 
A. 
Yes. During 2023-2025, the Company proposes to undertake two 
17 
projects to increase the CVO energy savings to 1.75 percent 
18 
by 2025.  
19 


--- Page 72 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
72 
 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposed continuation of the 
1 
CVO program. 
2 
A. 
To increase CVO energy savings during the upcoming period, 
3 
we will make the following two investments. First, to 
4 
address secondary system areas where there may be low 
5 
voltage, we need to reinforce the secondary system. That is, 
6 
we will upgrade and reconductor our secondary system to 
7 
allow the Company to adjust the voltage, while meeting 
8 
voltage 
range 
requirements. 
Second, 
we 
will 
adjust 
9 
transformers in area substations to allow settings to be 
10 
changed for voltage optimization, an O&M cost. This work 
11 
requires the transformer to be removed from service to work 
12 
on the internal equipment.  
13 
Q. 
Will these two projects increase the amount of savings 
14 
associated with CVO? 
15 
A. 
These efforts are expected to produce net energy savings 
16 
that will reduce the supply-side costs of the customer bill, 
17 
while further reducing GHG emissions.

iv. Advancing DSP Innovation 
19 
Q. 
What is meant by Grid Edge? 
20 


--- Page 73 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
73 
 
A. 
Grid edge is the point where utility infrastructure ends, 
1 
and the customer connects to the utility. Presently, our 
2 
customers draw power from and export power to the grid. In 
3 
the 
future, 
we 
see 
a 
substantial 
scaling 
of 
this 
4 
functionality requiring study and testing to confirm that 
5 
the grid can handle these flows.  
6 
Q. 
What is a grid edge laboratory? 
7 
A. 
A grid-edge laboratory is a physical and virtual space where 
8 
new and conceptual energy technologies, including software, 
9 
can be developed and subsequently tested for potential 
10 
deployment without any effect on operations. Mirror versions 
11 
of operating software are created within the laboratory.  
12 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s proposal related to the grid 
13 
edge technology laboratory. 
14 
A. 
The Company proposes to build a grid edge technology 
15 
laboratory that will collaborate with: (1) local community 
16 
advocate organizations; (2) clean energy technology vendors; 
17 
and (3) other utilities with large urban systems.  
18 
In the short term, this laboratory will allow Con Edison to 
19 
develop new tools and train employees in a simulated 
20 


--- Page 74 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
74 
 
environment. In the longer term, this laboratory will be 
1 
leveraged to develop the next horizon of operational 
2 
capabilities, for New York’s unique grid topology. 
3

Please continue. 
4 
A. 
As part of these investments, the Company proposes to 
5 
improve the hosting and registration process by developing

select favorable DER sites on its hosting capacity maps. 
8 
Second, the Company will expand its ability to monitor and 
9

10 
system operations. Third, the Company will develop software 
11 
tools to facilitate the smart inverter program discussed 
12 
above.

A. Energy Storage 
3 
Q. 
Please summarize Con Edison’s proposals to advance the use 
4 
and effectiveness of energy storage within its service 
5 
territory. 
6 
A.  First, we note that the Company is making these proposals 
7 
to help achieve the State’s clean energy goal of 6,000 MW 
8 
of energy storage by 2030. We estimate that approximately 
9 
3,000 MW of the energy storage will be located in our service 
10 
territory. Energy storage is crucial to achieving the clean 
11 
energy transition downstate, as the State implements its 
12 
policy of retiring in-City peaking generation and replacing 
13 
them with offshore wind facilities. As such, Con Edison is 
14 
taking steps to achieve this vision.  
15 
First, the Company proposes to create an energy storage 
16 
group to develop new products and delivery channels to 
17 
facilitate the installation, interconnection, and operation 
18 
of energy storage. This group, which requires additional 
19 
staffing, will expand the Company’s energy storage knowledge 
20 


--- Page 80 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
80 
 
base and help deploy energy storage installations in the

Q. 
What are the benefits of these projects? 
1 
A. 
The projects address cutting edge power quality challenges 
2 
that are not efficiently addressed by traditional equipment, 
3 
such as balancing the composition of VARs on the 
4 
distribution network. Certain electronic equipment (e.g., 
5 
those without a motor), including EV chargers and inductive 
6 
cooktops, are creating new power quality dynamics on the 
7 
system because of how they use electricity. Energy storage 
8 
systems have the potential to improve power quality 
9 
management. 
These 
projects 
will 
increase 
operational 
10 
flexibility through new controls for grid operations at the 
11 
substation and in the local distribution network / load 
12 
area.  
13 
Each project will also help develop new pathways for future 
14 
energy storage development. Implementing these projects will 
15 
help the Company develop key energy storage and DER 
16 
integration competencies around development, engineering 
17 
and design, procurement, construction, operation, and 
18 
maintenance of storage assets. 
 
19

includes

required

FERC

implementation. Any NYISO lessons learned can be shared with

between 2023 and 2025.

i. Non-Wires Solutions 
3 
Q.  What is an NWS? 
4 
A.  An NWS is a cost-effective portfolio of non-traditional, 
5 
typically 
customer-side 
solutions, 
that 
enable 
the 
6 
elimination or deferral of a traditional asset that would 
7 
be required to meet a reliability need.23 The Company 
8 
implements NWS in identified areas of locational need to 
9 
defer or eliminate traditional infrastructure solutions. We 
10 
develop NWS portfolios that are generally comprised of 
11 
different DER solutions. In addition to deferring or 
12 
eliminating the traditional solution, benefits include 
13 
decreased energy and capacity costs from the wholesale 
14 
market, 
reductions 
in 
GHG 
emissions, 
marginal 
cost 
15 
reductions 
to 
upstream 
transmission 
and 
distribution 
16

3

4

5 
gas system infrastructure costs. NPAs are consistent with 
6 
State energy policy as expressed in the CLCPA and the Climate 
7 
Action

Draft 
Scoping 
Plan’s 
emphasis 
on 
8 
decarbonizing the State’s building stock and vision for 
9 
“downsizing”27 the gas system. In addition, the Company’s 
10 
current rate plan required that the Company develop a 
11 
framework for NPAs, which was filed in September 2020.

16 
recently, in December 2021, the Company filed a Targeted NPA

Q.  What other accounting treatments does the Company propose 
4

6

8

11

12

13 
associated with the project up to that point as a regulatory 
14 
asset. A single amortization period for the NPA portfolio 
15 
also provides administrative and accounting consistency and 
16 
simplicity.

Q.  Is the Company proposing an incentive mechanism for NPA 
18 
projects?  
19 


--- Page 98 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
98 
 
A.  Yes. The Company is proposing a performance incentive 
1 
mechanism designed to drive NPAs that provide meaningful 
2 
achievement and net benefits to customers. This mechanism 
3 
mirrors the approach currently in place for NWS, where 
4 
shareholders retain a portion of the net societal benefits 
5 
of each NWS, as measured using the BCAH. The four key 
6 
components of the incentive proposal are:  
7 
1. An Initial Incentive where shareholders retain 30 
8 
percent of the Initial Net Benefits, and customers 
9 
retain 70 percent as determined by the SCT performed 
10 
prior to NPA implementation;  
11 
2. A bounded, Cost-Containment Incentive that rewards 
12 
the Company for reducing costs during NPA 
13 
implementation or penalizes it for cost overruns, 
14 
with a cap such that the Final Incentive cannot 
15 
exceed 50 percent of the Initial Net Benefits and a 
16 
floor of $0;  
17 
3. A provision to address a situation in which an NPA 
18 
project is not able to defer or eliminate the 
19 
traditional project as initially intended; and  
20 


--- Page 99 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
99 
 
4. Provisions for a change in NPA Portfolio sizing needs 
1 
for an active NPA project. 
2 
The Company’s proposed performance incentive structure 
3 
represents a reasonable distribution of benefits because gas 
4 
customers receive a majority of the net societal benefits. 
5 
Moreover, the incentive encourages the Company to pursue NPA 
6 
projects to maximize net societal benefits. Finally, the 
7 
inclusion of the cost-containment provisions rewards the 
8 
Company for cost-effective implementation of the NPA.

Q.  How will the Initial Incentive be determined?  
3 
A.  The Initial Incentive is determined by the Initial Net 
4 
Benefits, which are simply the sum of the NPV of the societal 
5 
benefits less the NPV of the societal costs. Initial Net 
6 
Benefits will be calculated at the time when the Company 
7 
submits its BCA to Staff. Similar to NWS projects, the 
8 
Company will submit its BCA to Staff when it has entered 
9 
into contracts with solution providers for the entire 
10 
portfolio or is confident in the costs of the NPA portfolio.  
11 
Q.  Can you describe the mechanism of the cost containment 
12 
incentive?  
13

A.  The Final Incentive will be determined by the actual project 
1 
spending, subject to the Cost Containment Incentive. The 
2 
Final Incentive is, in a simple case, the sum of the Initial 
3 
Incentive and 50 percent of the difference between planned 
4 
costs and actual costs. The Final Incentive is bounded such 
5 
that it will not exceed 50 percent of the Initial Net 
6 
Benefits and will not be negative.  
7 
Q.  Over what period will the Company collect the NPA incentive?

10 
The NPA Deferral/Elimination Period begins when the Initial 
11

12 
that the NPA portfolio delays or eliminates the traditional 
13 
infrastructure build, as determined at the time of setting 
14 
the Initial Net Benefits. The Company will begin collecting 
15 
the Initial Incentive once 70 percent of the NPA portfolio 
16 
is operational, measured in Dth/Day of load relief on the 
17 
Design Day. The incentive will be amortized over the 
18 
remaining NPA Deferral/Elimination Period (e.g., if 70 
19 
percent of NPA portfolio is operational in the third year 
20

out of a five-year Deferral/Elimination Period, the 
1 
incentive will be recovered over the remaining two years of 
2 
the Deferral/Elimination Period). The Company will collect 
3 
all incentive payments, inclusive of the overall pre-tax 
4 
rate of return, through the Monthly Rate Adjustment (“MRA”), 
5 
using the same cost recovery mechanism described in the 
6 
Company’s current Rate Plan for other program costs. For 
7 
clarity, the Deferral/Elimination Period when the NPA 
8

B. Electric Vehicles 
15 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s Electric Vehicle Charging 
16 
Make-Ready program. 
17 
A. 
The Electric Vehicle Charging Make-Ready program, authorized 
18 
by the Order Establishing Electric Vehicle Infrastructure 
19 
Make-Ready Program and Other Programs, encourages developers 
20 
to install increasing amounts of EV chargers by providing 
21 


--- Page 128 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
128 
 
incentives of up to 100 percent of the costs of making EV 
1 
charging sites ready for charger installation. The program 
2 
incentivizes the installation of infrastructure supporting 
3 
two types of chargers: slower Level 2 (“L2”) plugs which are 
4 
typically found at multifamily dwellings, workplaces, 
5 
parking garages, curbsides and at commercial areas, and 
6 
faster Direct Current Fast Chargers (“DCFC”) that are 
7 
typically found at areas such as urban charging hubs serving 
8 
multiple customer types. Make ready costs include utility-
9 
owned 
equipment, 
such 
as 
traditional 
distribution 
10 
infrastructure, overhead service lines, and utility meters, 
11 
as well as customer-owned equipment and costs such as 
12 
conductors, trenching, and panels needed for the station.  
13 
Q. 
How will program costs authorized by the Order Establishing 
14 
Electric Vehicle Infrastructure Make-Ready Program and Other 
15 
Programs be recovered during the Rate Plan? 
16 
A. 
Expenses for customer-owned make-ready work will be 
17 
recovered as a regulatory asset over a period of 15 years 
18 
and collected through an existing surcharge mechanism. 
19 
Expenses for program implementation will be recovered as a 
20 


--- Page 129 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
129 
 
regulatory asset over a period of five years. Utility-owned 
1 
new business and make-ready work, including work related to 
2 
future-proofing utility infrastructure, will be treated as 
3 
capitalized plant in service (these expenditures are 
4 
discussed further in the EIOP Testimony). Please refer to 
5 
the Accounting Panel for further detail on cost recovery 
6

VII. Promoting Clean Energy Outcomes 
3 
Q.  Please describe the mechanisms by which the Commission 
4 
encourages the Company to promote clean energy outcomes. 
5 
A. 
As part of the REV Proceeding, the Commission adopted 
6 
various mechanisms to align utilities’ business interests 
7 
with clean energy policy objectives. The Commission 
8 
authorized 
the 
development 
of 
and 
adopted 
Earnings 
9 
Adjustment 
Mechanisms 
(“EAMs”) 
to 
provide 
meaningful 
10 
economic incentives for achievement of various policy 
11 
outcomes, such as reducing peak demands for electricity and, 
12 
more recently, natural gas, and achievement of certain 
13 
programmatic targets.  
14 
Q. 
What EAMs are currently in place for Con Edison?  
15 
A. 
The Company’s current rate plan consists of seven EAMs: 
16 
 Share 
the 
Savings 
(“STS”): 
Incentivizes 
17 
overachievement of non-LMI NENY EE goals and driving 
18 
unit cost efficiency; 
19 


--- Page 131 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
131 
 
 Deeper Energy Efficiency (“Deeper EE”): Incentivizes 
1 
driving EE and electrification savings to more 
2 
challenging and more expensive measures (e.g., non-
3 
lighting measures); 
4 
 Beneficial 
Electrification: 
Incentivizes 
driving 
5 
higher adoption of heat pumps and electric vehicles to 
6 
electrify end uses;  
7 
 DER Utilization: Incentivizes interconnecting MWs of 
8 
DER (e.g., solar and battery storage) above forecasts 
9 
to reduce GHG emissions and develop the customer sided 
10 
resources for a more resilient and flexible grid; 
11 
 Electric System Peak: Incentivizes driving overall 
12 
system peak reductions beyond forecasts to reduce GHG 
13 
emissions, mitigate the need for system coincident 
14 
infrastructure, and reduce stress on the grid during 
15 
system coincident peak times to improve reliability; 
16 
 Locational System Relief Value (“LSRV”)) Load Factor: 
17 
Incentivizes improving the load factor, the ratio of a 
18 
network’s consumption divided by the area’s MW capacity 
19 


--- Page 132 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
132 
 
multiplied by 8,760 for the numbers of hours in year; 
1 
and 
2 
 Gas System Peak: Incentivizes driving firm gas system 
3 
peak reductions to reduce the Company’s gas supply 
4 
needs. 
5 
Q. 
Please describe the Company’s performance under those EAMs.  
6 
A. 
In 2020, the Company exceeded its minimum targets for the 
7 
Share the Savings, Deeper EE, Electric System Peak and Gas 
8 
System Peak EAMs. The Company achieved these outcomes 
9 
through launching the new Clean Heat program, building 
10 
awareness of the Company’s programs, and working with market 
11 
participants and customers to complete more EE and heating 
12 
electrification projects. The Company did not achieve the 
13 
minimum targets for the DER Utilization and LSRV Load Factor 
14 
EAMs. Data related to Beneficial Electrification is still 
15 
under review. The Company expects to file the annual report 
16 
on its 2021 EAM achievements on March 31, 2022.

Q. 
Describe which policy objectives the proposed EAMs support.  
13 
A.  The Company’s proposed EAMs in this testimony build on 
14 
lessons learned and take new state policy objectives into 
15 
account. The Company’s proposed EAMs incentivize utility 
16 
activity to accelerate and overachieve important policy 
17 
objectives, such as: (1) growing EE and DER; (2) lowering 
18 
electric and gas system peak to achieve Statewide delivery 
19 
system efficiencies; (3) reducing GHG emissions; (4) driving 
20 


--- Page 134 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC.

134 
 
savings and benefits to LMI customers and Disadvantaged 
1

2 
by promoting beneficial electrification, which will move 
3 
customers away from fossil fuels. 
4 
Q. 
Describe the Company’s general approach to target setting.  
5 
A.  The core concept for EAMs is the principle that they should 
6 
incentivize Company activity above and beyond expected 
7 
achievement due to normal course of business (a “baseline”) 
8 
to accelerate achievement of policy objectives. Given this 
9 
principle, the Company generally follows one of two 
10 
approaches to establish the baseline: 1) the baseline 
11 
represents planned achievement according to Company filings, 
12 
investment plans, or mandated targets or 2) the baseline 
13 
represents the extrapolation of a trendline based on 
14 
historic performance. Once the Company establishes the 
15 
baseline, it develops minimum, midpoint, and maximum targets 
16 
that are both meaningful and achievable. Specific targets 
17 
and details on how they were developed for each EAM are 
18 
outlined in Exhibit__(CES-9), “EAM Exhibit”. 
19

139 
 
CLCPA goals, and/or drives above “business as usual” 
1 
achievement of outcomes critical to achieving the clean 
2 
energy transition. Second, the Company assessed the lessons 
3 
learned from each EAM to test whether the EAM is 1) 
4 
effectively drives the intended outcomes, 2) is measurable, 
5 
and 3) the Company has some degree of influence. 
6 
Q. 
Please continue. 
7 
A. 
In terms of lessons learned, the Company’s current EAMs and 
8 
associated performance informed the EAM proposal in this 
9 
rate filing in five ways.  
10 
First, the Electric System Peak Reduction EAM benefits 
11 
society as it reduces GHG emissions from “peaker” plants, 
12 
which have higher emission intensities, and help to reduce 
13 
capacity costs. The metric is sufficiently straightforward 
14 
in its calculation and the Company’s actions and influence 
15 
to drive MWs of system peak reduction are appropriately 
16 
linked to EAM achievement. We propose to continue this 
17 
metric with minor modifications to how targets are set.  
18 


--- Page 140 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
140 
 
Second, the Share the Savings (STS) and Deeper EE EAMs are 
1 
metrics that together demonstrated effectiveness at driving 
2 
utility performance against NENY goals. The STS metric drove 
3 
both overachievement of EE goals and unit cost efficiencies. 
4 
The Deeper EE metric effectively complemented STS to 
5 
mitigate the disincentive from STS to drive more expensive 
6 
but important measures and effectively pushed the EE and 
7 
electrification portfolios in this direction. We propose to 
8 
retain these metrics with modifications such as narrowing 
9 
their respective scopes to less mature sources of EE and 
10 
building electrification savings and excluding gas devices 
11 
savings to better align them with current State priorities 
12 
and the dynamics of a transforming energy market.  
13 
Third, the Beneficial Electrification and DER Utilization 
14 
EAMs support key State policy objectives. The Company 
15 
proposes modifications to the DER Utilization EAM to address 
16 
uncertainties in the solar and battery storage markets in 
17 
target setting. In addition, the Company proposes to narrow 
18 
the Beneficial Electrification EAM to electric vehicles and 
19 
remove heat pumps to reduce overlap with the proposed Deep 
20 


--- Page 141 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
141 
 
Savings EAM (which will reward overachievement of heat pump 
1 
targets).  
2 
Fourth, the Gas Peak EAM encourages the Company to focus on 
3 
winter gas peak management to reduce upstream supply 
4 
obligations and increase the efficiency of the gas system. 
5 
The 
metric 
is 
sufficiently 
straightforward 
in 
its 
6 
calculation and the Company’s actions and influence are 
7 
appropriately linked to EAM achievement. Therefore, the 
8 
Company proposes to retain the Gas Peak EAM without 
9 
modification.  
10 
Finally, the LSRV EAM fell short of achieving the intended 
11 
outcomes and the Company proposes to discontinue this EAM. 
12 
The current LSRV metric disincentivizes many kinds of EE and 
13 
solar technologies which the Company believes are core 
14 
priorities for achieving CLCPA goals. The LSRV EAM metric 
15 
measures the ratio of a network’s consumption divided by the 
16 
area’s MW capacity multiplied by 8,760 (number of hours in 
17 
a year). As a result, this EAM rewards the Company for 
18 
limiting solar development in LSRV areas, as solar reduces 
19 
electric consumption, but only reduces peak demand in 
20

142 
 
networks with peaks coincident with solar production.

integration 
of 
customer 
sided 
solutions 
to 
reduce

8 
i. Electric Share the Savings (“Electric STS”)  
9 
Q. 
Please describe the goal of the Electric STS metric and how 
10 
it is aligned with policy objectives. 
11 
A. 
The 
Electric 
STS 
metric 
encourages 
the 
Company 
to 
12 
overachieve non-LMI electric EE targets while driving unit 
13 
cost efficiencies and focus the Company on further growing 
14

15 
Growing savings in these other areas, is important for 
16 
achievement of the CLCPA’s goal to drive an 85% reduction 
17 
in GHG emissions by 2050 and to reduce 22 million tons of 
18 
carbon through EE and electrification. 
19

2 
electric efficiency savings that are acquired through the 
3 
Company’s non-LMI electric EE programs.  
4

5 
EE savings from the Electric STS EAM?

7

8 
fixtures 
through 
the

and 
9

While lighting EE is an important component of our program, 
11 
the Company’s non-LMI residential lighting programs have 
12

13 
the Company proposes to focus the Electric STS metric on

rewarding the savings from developing less mature EE 
15 
savings. Excluding non-LMI residential lighting savings from 
16 
the Electric STS metric incents the Company to evolve its 
17 
EE portfolio and focus on other sources of EE.

is

construct 
not

ii. Gas Share the Savings (“Gas STS”) 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the goal of the Gas STS metric and how it 
4 
is aligned with policy objectives. 
5 
A. 
The Gas STS metric encourages the Company to overachieve the 
6 
non-LMI gas EE targets set by NENY while driving unit cost 
7 
efficiencies. It is aligned with the CLCPA’s goal to drive 
8 
an 85% reduction in GHG emissions by 2050 and to eliminate 
9 
22 million tons of carbon emissions through EE and 
10 
electrification. 
11 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company will measure this EAM.  
12 
A. 
The Company will measure Gas STS achievement EAM by 
13 
calculating the lifetime EE savings acquired through the 
14 
Company’s non-LMI NENY programs excluding savings from 
15 
efficient gas-consuming space heating equipment, water 
16 
heating equipment, and cooking equipment and appliances, 
17 
referred to here as “gas devices.”  
18 


--- Page 147 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
147 
 
Q. 
Why does the Company propose to exclude gas space heating, 
1 
water 
heating, 
and 
cooking 
equipment 
and 
appliance 
2 
efficiency savings from the Gas STS EAM? 
3 
A. 
To achieve the CLCPA emission goals, the State will reduce 
4 
reliance on EE savings from efficient gas devices and 
5 
emphasize building electrification savings. Because gas 
6 
heating and cooking equipment often have long useful lives 
7 
(15 years or more is common) incentivizing a customer to 
8 
install, for example, a new, more efficient, gas fired 
9 
boiler today could delay that customer’s transition to 
10 
electrified end uses and continue to produce emissions 
11 
that are targeted by the CLCPA.  
12 
At the same time, the Company recognizes that EE programs 
13 
should continue to support customers who find 
14 
electrification difficult in the near term because the 
15 
electric heating technology available today does not allow 
16 
for a simple retrofit of certain building typologies. For 
17 
example, tall buildings heated with steam produced by 
18 
their gas boiler cannot easily replace the existing 
19 
heating equipment with a heat pump. In these instances, 
20 


--- Page 148 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
148 
 
the Company believes customers should pursue the most 
1 
efficient gas heat and hot water equipment possible which 
2 
the Company would continue to incentivize. Considering the 
3 
tension laid out above, the Company proposes to exclude 
4 
gas-device efficiency (space heating, water heating, and 
5 
cooking equipment and appliances) from the Gas STS metric 
6 
to encourage the gas portfolio to place greater emphasis 
7 
on non-gas device EE activities, such as building envelope 
8 
upgrades.  
9 
Q. 
How will the Company calculate the baseline for this EAM, 
10 
including data sources the Company will use for baseline 
11 
development?  
12 
A. 
The Gas STS metric is formulaic (see Exhibit __ (CES-9) for 
13 
details). Like the Electric STS metric above, the Company 
14 
proposes that the Company must achieve its annual NENY non-
15 
LMI gas annual MMBtu goal to earn on the Gas STS metric. The 
16 
Company has also proposed a scaling formula in Exhibit __ 
17 
(CES-9) for the Gas STS metric to account for possible 
18 
changes to NENY budgets and targets from the upcoming 
19

CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC.

interim review or other Commission action in the NENY 
1 
proceeding.

3

Midpoint, and maximum targets are not applicable to the Gas

Q. 
Why does the Company propose to exclude LMI savings from the 
16 
Electric and Gas STS metrics? 
17 
A. 
As LMI building owners have less access to capital than 
18 
market rate customers and have constraints on recouping 
19 


--- Page 150 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC.

150 
 
investments due to appropriate rent regulations, the 
1 
financial incentives for EE and building electrification

upgrades are more important to implement projects. As a

4

5

6

7

iii. Deep Savings  
10 
Q. 
Please describe the goal of the Deep Savings metric and how 
11 
it aligns with policy objectives. 
12 
A. 
The Deep Savings metric encourages overachievement of the 
13 
NENY heat pump targets and grows EE savings coming from more 
14 
expensive, challenging measures, such as building envelope, 
15 
advanced controls, and waste heat recovery measures, which 
16 
the Electric and Gas STS metrics’ focus on reducing unit 
17 
costs can disincentivize. For example, the Company’s 
18 
budgeted non-LMI gas unit cost for 2023 is $39 per annual 
19 
MMBtu saved. Going into 2022, the Company is increasing its 
20 


--- Page 151 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
151 
 
incentive rate for envelope measures, where most of the 
1 
associated savings come from saving natural gas, to $200 per 
2 
MMBtu saved to spur additional activity as the Company has 
3 
seen its existing incentive rate was insufficient to drive 
4 
demand. The new envelope incentive rate will reduce earnings 
5 
under the Electric and Gas STS metrics despite being a 
6 
critical source of savings to develop to achieve CLCPA. 
7 
Achieving both the building electrification required by 
8 
CLCPA’s long-term goals and mitigating the impacts of future 
9 
winter peak growth will require driving deep EE upgrades, 
10 
like building envelope improvements.  
11 
Q. 
Has the Deeper EE EAM metric in place for the 2020 – 2022 
12 
Rate Period been effective at achieving its goal developing 
13 
new sources of savings to position the Company to continue 
14 
to drive EE and electrification savings at scale over the 
15 
medium to long-term?  
16 
A. 
Yes. This metric has been effective developing the more 
17 
challenging and expensive measures that are not strictly 
18 
necessary for achieving goals today but will be critical to 
19 
continue driving savings in pursuit of the CLCPA’s long-term 
20 


--- Page 152 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
152 
 
goals. For example, the Company has increased its share of 
1 
non-lighting savings in the electric portfolio from 28% in 
2 
2019, the year before the Deeper EE EAM was put in place, 
3 
to roughly 56% in 2021.  
4 
Q. 
Does the Company propose to modify the Deeper EE metric from 
5 
the 2020 – 2022 Rate Period? 
6 
A. 
Yes. The Company propose to narrow the definition of Deep 
7 
to focus more on the most challenging and expensive, but 
8 
important, savings sources. As the Company transitions to 
9 
an electrified building stock, building envelope and 
10 
advanced control measures will drive additional energy 
11 
savings, mitigate the peak impact from electrifying heat and 
12 
hot water, and develop buildings into grid resources to 
13 
manage demand.  
14

The Company will measure the Deep Savings metric by 
16

17 
programs and from other priority EE measures (e.g., building 
18

envelope) that the Company proposes to include in this 
1 
metric. See Exhibit __ (CES-9) for more details.

15

16 
the Company’s LMI and non-LMI NENY EE programs. Savings 
17 
acquired within Disadvantaged Communities will be counted 
18 
towards Disadvantaged Community achievement. 
19 


--- Page 155 ---

Q. 
Why does the Company propose to measure annual MMBtu savings 
1 
instead of lifetime savings as proposed in the STS and Deep 
2 
Savings metrics? 
3 
A. 
It is important to drive EE savings and the associated bill 
4 
savings, improvements in air quality, and improved comfort 
5 
for LMI and Disadvantaged Community customers in the near 
6 
term. As such, the Company proposes to measure this EAM in 
7 
annual MMBtu, which will put a greater emphasis on 
8 
maximizing near-term savings.

10

13 
Communities metric as that discussed above for the non-LMI 
14 
Gas STS metric.

16

17

18 
in the Low-Income Customers & Disadvantaged Communities 
19 


--- Page 156 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
156 
 
metric because it believes lighting measures are not a 
1 
mature EE upgrade for LMI customers and lighting upgrades 
2 
are a meaningful way to drive bill reductions for these 
3 
customers. Many LMI customers are renters and do not have 
4 
ownership or control over many of the in-unit appliances 
5 
that contribute to their energy bills, limiting the range 
6 
of EE upgrades they can make. In addition to the greater 
7 
control offered by LED lighting, it is one of the few 
8 
measures that renters can continue to benefit from when 
9 
moving to a new house or apartment. Customers can take their 
10 
LEDs with them unlike refrigerators, for example, which 
11 
typically are the property of the building owner.

device efficiency savings. The Company has also proposed a 
16

to overachieve the 35% of benefits of investment goal. The

Q.

4

5

6

NENY 
period 
13 
achievement in its December 2021 SEEP filing which it 
14

16

18 
proceeding. For example, given the accomplishments in Clean

v. Demand Response 
3 
Q. 
Please describe the goal of the Demand Response EAM and how 
4 
it aligns with policy objectives. 
5 
A.  The DR metric is intended to drive significant participation 
6 
from smaller customers in pay-for-participation DR programs 
7 
(CSRP and Term-DLM) beyond the programs’ expected organic 
8 
growth. This EAM promotes grid flexibility in support of 
9 
CLCPA’s goal to achieve 100% clean energy by 2040 by 
10 
developing a larger and more reliable Demand Response 
11 
resource that the Company can call on to reduce network peak 
12 
demand and during network contingencies.  
13 
Q. 
Please explain why the Company proposes to introduce a DR 
14 
EAM. 
15 
A. 
The Company proposes this EAM to achieve greater growth in 
16 
small customer participation, a largely untapped customer 
17 
segment. Prior to the deployment of AMI, customers with less 
18 
than 500 kW peak demand had to pay for communicating interval 
19 


--- Page 160 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL 
 
160 
 
meters that are required to enroll in the Company’s DR 
1 
program. This requirement hindered small customers from 
2 
participating in the DR programs because few customers 
3 
proactively made this investment. AMI deployment has newly 
4 
expanded DR eligibility to all customers segments, but most 
5 
small commercial and residential customers are not yet 
6 
engaged with the DR program. Given the opportunity for 
7 
growth in customer participation AMI has unlocked, the 
8 
Company proposes this EAM to accelerate small customer 
9 
enrollment beyond organic growth.  
10 
Q. 
Please describe how the Company will measure this EAM.  
11 
A. 
The Company will measure the DR EAM by using DRMS data to 
12 
report customer count in a similar manner to the DR Annual 
13 
Report. This metric will measure a segment of the total 
14 
number of DR participants. The metric will take the number 
15 
of SC1 and SC2 customers enrolled that achieved a 
16 
performance factor of at least 25% in at least one event. 
17 
This methodology will count customers who not only enroll 
18 
but also provide positive load relief reductions. 
19 


--- Page 161 ---
CONSOLIDATED EDISON COMPANY OF NEW YORK, INC. 
CUSTOMER ENERGY SOLUTIONS PANEL

Q.

1 
as the threshold for measurement of the EAM? 
2 
A. 
The Company sought to establish a minimum threshold to 
3 
verify the customers are providing demand reduction but 
4 
avoid adverse impacts from customers unfamiliar with demand 
5 
response 
potentially 
misjudging 
their 
enrollment 
6 
(participating in DR will be a new way of engaging with 
7 
energy for many smaller customers, likely requiring time to 
8 
calibrate their enrollment and interest in the programs). 
9 
25% is the minimum performance required for an Aggregator 
10 
to receive a Reservation Payment as outlined in the Electric 
11 
Tariff. As a result, the Company proposes to use 25% for 
12 
this purpose as well. 
13

16

18

19 
1.7. For each subsequent rate year, the baseline will be 
20

17 
BTU/gallon 
of 
gasoline 
factor 
is 
sourced 
from 
the 
18

4

5
